SmartTranscripts:

Human Services Committee - 2025-03-11

Meetings

  • SB 1476 — ABLE Act alignment and program improvements (00:14:12)
    State Treasurer Erick Russell supports updating ABLE CT to match federal law, broaden eligibility age to 46 in 2026, authorize covering account fees, and ensure ABLE savings are disregarded for all means-tested programs.
  • SB 1476 — ABLE Act Q&A (Rep. Jay Case) (00:18:37)
    Rep. Case questions ABLE qualified expenses and emphasizes how ABLE accounts prevent spend-downs for disabled individuals while allowing savings and tax benefits.
  • SB 1476 — ABLE Act Q&A (Sen. Matt Lesser) (00:20:22)
    Sen. Lesser asks about adopting federal changes by reference and what future changes might be anticipated; Treasurer Russell cites age expansion and desire to avoid repeated statutory updates.
  • SB 1482 — Elderly nutrition (DSS Commissioner overview) (00:23:29)
    Commissioner Barton Reeves outlines SNAP-related efforts for older adults, existing ESAP waiver, expanded outreach, Meals on Wheels participation, and collaboration with Aging & Disability Services.
  • SB 1475 — Restaurant Meals Program (DSS Commissioner overview) (00:25:52)
    DSS reviews the Restaurant Meals Program option for SNAP recipients, notes limited uptake in other states, costs and administrative lift, and invites policy dialogue on feeding unhoused residents.
  • SB 1471 — Community-based memory care pilot (DSS Commissioner) (00:28:59)
    DSS expresses willingness to work on a memory care pilot but flags Medicaid constraints on targeted catchment areas; proposes continued collaboration with Housing.
  • SB 11 — Prescription drug bill (Sections 7–9: Nursing home 80% direct care; emergency medical; Husky C assets) (00:30:02)
    DSS raises concerns about mandating 80% direct care spend due to operational realities; urges adhering to federal emergency medical definitions to protect reimbursement; details projected costs of phasing out Husky C asset limits.
  • HB 7192 — Drug Task Force recommendations (DSS Commissioner) (00:35:00)
    DSS supports implementing bipartisan drug task force recommendations to improve affordability and access.
  • HB 7188 — Homelessness and the CHESS program (DSS Commissioner) (00:35:37)
    DSS relays study findings recommending sunsetting CHESS in current form due to provider capacity and lack of vouchers; proposes moving voucher holders to DMHAS and coordinating supports.
  • HB 7191 — Medicaid rate increases planning and sustainability (DSS Commissioner) (00:38:00)
    DSS suggests using Phase 1 & 2 rate study and landscape analysis to guide increases in a coordinated, phased manner with OPM and stakeholders.
  • SB 1251 — Implementer: Domestic violence benefit shift and cost-sharing changes (DSS Commissioner) (00:39:13)
    DSS describes challenges administering the one-time DV benefit and supports transferring administration to CCADV; highlights Section 13 home care cost-sharing cap changes and related budget savings.
  • HB 7188 — CHESS program: Committee questions (Chair Gilchrist) (00:43:10)
    Chair Gilchrist probes 1915(i) waiver structure and potential reconfiguration, expresses concern about sunsetting CHESS and urges exploring other state models before ending the program.
  • SB 1475 — Restaurant Meals Program: Committee questions (Sen. Lesser) (00:54:39)
    Sen. Lesser cites Massachusetts’ targeted RMP including food trucks serving diverse communities and questions taking options off the table for unhoused residents.
  • SB 1475 — Restaurant Meals Program uptake barriers (Rep. Jay Case) (01:08:09)
    Rep. Case details prior outreach to small businesses and systemic technology/audit burdens that discourage participation, especially among small shops.
  • SB 1251 — DV benefit program realignment (Rep. Jay Case) (01:10:41)
    Rep. Case supports moving the DV cash assistance program to CCADV, citing expertise and better targeting of funds while acknowledging DSS limitations.
  • SB 11 — Nursing home direct care ratio concerns (Rep. Jay Case) (01:12:31)
    Rep. Case challenges an 80% direct care mandate, citing facility financial realities, private pay dynamics, and the need for capital improvements and holistic care costs.
  • HB 7188 — Preserve and strengthen CHESS (Rep. Susan Johnson) (01:20:36)
    Rep. Johnson argues against eliminating CHESS, urging better coordination and leveraging redevelopment to create housing plus wraparound services for older adults and vulnerable populations.
  • HB 7189 — Medicaid cost savings: Nurse delegation study and safety (Rep. Caitlin Sheikh) (01:23:22)
    Rep. Sheikh questions expanding medication delegation in nursing facilities, raises legal liability and patient safety concerns, and calls for DPH and nursing stakeholder involvement.
  • HB 7191 — FQHC rebasing and sustainability (Yvette Highsmith, CHC, Inc.) (01:35:32)
    Highsmith testifies that FQHCs are underpaid relative to cost-based federal requirements, losing per-visit revenue and relying on philanthropy; supports rate rebasing and scheduled updates.
  • SB 11 — Prescription drug access & affordability (OHS Commissioner Gifford) (01:39:30)
    OHS supports capping generic/off-patent price growth at inflation, exploring Canadian importation feasibility, and implementing PBM transparency reforms; cites estimated $9M in wholesale savings.
  • SB 1477 — Covered Connecticut Trust Fund (OHS Commissioner Gifford) (01:42:13)
    OHS appreciates intent to promote Covered CT sustainability but opposes creating a non-lapsing fund absent identified funding in the Governor’s budget.
  • SB 1480 — Private equity and REIT ownership limits (OHS Commissioner Gifford) (01:44:03)
    OHS supports enhanced transaction transparency and AG oversight to address financialization, encourages harmonizing SB 1480 with broader state transaction review reforms.
  • SB 1472 — DDS regional centers (CT Nonprofit Alliance) (01:54:17)
    Ben Shaiken opposes reopening DDS regional centers, noting the nonprofit sector serves the vast majority of residents at lower cost; urges investment in community services.
  • HB 7191 — Behavioral health clinic rates (CT Nonprofit Alliance) (01:54:54)
    Alliance urges prioritizing underfunded outpatient behavioral health rates identified as severely below peer states; requests a $40M increase and regular reviews.
  • SB 1251 — DMV adaptive driver training transfer; SB 1482; HB 7190 (ADS Commissioner Porter) (01:59:27)
    ADS backs returning adaptive driver training to DMV for integrated licensing, supports ongoing SNAP senior nutrition collaborations, and notes existing comprehensive studies on senior needs.
  • HB 7191 — Fair Haven CHC leadership support (02:05:35)
    Maggie Moffitt and Marta Moret describe philanthropic stopgaps and research showing community health centers’ efficacy; urge rebasing and sustainable Medicaid rates.
  • SB 1251 — Health Care Advocate office scope (OHA) (02:13:21)
    Kathleen Holt warns against merging consumer advocacy with provider-focused behavioral health advocate functions; calls for clear firewall to preserve OHA’s consumer mission.
  • HB 7191 — CHC, Inc. CEO testimony (Mark Masselli) (02:23:01)
    Masselli supports cost-based reimbursement fixes and warns of emerging federal risks that may require alternative state-backed safety-net care models if federal funds are restricted.
  • SB 805, SB 1479, SB 1480 — Ombudsman recommendations (02:26:51)
    State LTC Ombudsman supports transparency and accountability in facility spending, curbs on mandatory arbitration, and restrictions on private equity/REIT ownership tied to quality concerns.
  • HB 7191 — Emergency physicians support indexing & access (02:34:50)
    Dr. Gregory Shangold urges MEI-based updates over Medicare-tied rates, citing physician payment cuts and access risks; emphasizes ED boarding task force recommendations.
  • HB 7189 — Cap temporary nurse staffing agency rates (02:41:05)
    John Ananth Raj supports capping agency fees at 200% of median wages, citing quality issues, staffing instability, and cost pressures on Medicaid nursing home care.
  • HB 7191 — Medication administration in home health (Rep. Rebecca Martinez) (02:46:03)
    Rep. Martinez calls for raising medication administration rates, restoring prior add-ons, and aligning with Massachusetts to ensure nurse safety and patient access.
  • SB 1483 (PCMH Plus) and HB 7191 — Policy analysis (Ellen Andrews) (02:50:27)
    CT Health Policy Project urges ending PCMH Plus due to poor results and implementing Medicaid rate increases, especially for behavioral health, per the rate studies.
  • SB 11 — PBM/340B testimony (CHA) (02:54:32)
    CHA supports 340B contract pharmacy protections but asks to align definitions to include hospitals, citing underpayment and uncompensated care burdens.
  • SB 11 — Stop-loss concerns (CBIA) (02:57:46)
    CBIA opposes stop-loss mandates for small employers, warning of increased aggregate attachment points, ERISA issues, and potential loss of coverage options.
  • HB 7191 — Fair Haven CHC board testimony (Luis Luna) (03:02:26)
    Luis Luna describes CHC losses and denial of rate increases despite expansion; urges adopting rate rebasing and COLA mechanisms.
  • HB 7191 — Home health & nurse delegation (CAHCH) (03:06:15)
    Tracy Wodatch urges accelerating increases, including all home health billing codes (esp. medication administration), and eliminating subsequent-visit penalties.
  • SB 807 — Husky C asset limits (public testimony) (03:09:34)
    Richard Famiglietti supports phasing out Husky C asset limits and highlights remaining income-limit discrimination impacting disabled residents.
  • HB 7192 — Bipartisan drug task force recommendations (Sen. Jeff Gordon) (03:13:23)
    Sen. Gordon outlines PBM fiduciary duties, transparency, patient access, countering consolidation, and addressing drug shortages/importation pathways.
  • HB 7192 / SB 11 — PBM transparency (independent pharmacist) (03:23:34)
    Ed Schreiner supports ending spread pricing, de-linking PBM compensation, and protecting pharmacies; details unsustainable contract terms and insulin formulary shifts.
  • HB 7192 — PBM reforms (Rep. Tracy Marra) (03:30:09)
    Rep. Marra emphasizes wholesale acquisition cost-based reimbursement and fiduciary standards, citing other states’ precedents and supply chain integrity.
  • HB 7191 — Patient & development perspectives (Fair Haven CHC) (03:49:33)
    Karen Namaia recounts Fair Haven’s growth, payer mix, and out-of-compliance cost-based mandates; urges passage to sustain care for tens of thousands.
  • HB 7191 — Dental rates & Fair Health data (CT State Dental Association) (03:54:54)
    CSDA supports increasing Medicaid dental rates using Fair Health data for zip code specificity; warns of reduced services at CHCs due to low reimbursement.
  • SB 11 / HB 7192 / SB 1479 / SB 1480 — Consumer advocacy (CCAG) (03:59:01)
    CCAG supports price growth caps, importation assessment, stop-loss protections, arbitration limits, and private equity oversight; opposes excessive PBM leverage.
  • HB 7191 — Clinical leadership support (Fair Haven CHC) (04:03:09)
    Dr. Emmanuel Martinez Alcaraz urges investment to retain providers and integrate medical, dental, and behavioral health services for complex patients.
  • HB 7191 — Chiropractic services access (Dr. George Curry) (04:06:30)
    Supports increased chiropractic Medicaid rates to expand access and reduce opioid use and costly interventions.
  • HB 7191 — Optometry parity (Dr. Brian Lynch) (04:10:05)
    Calls for eliminating the 90% reimbursement rule versus ophthalmology and establishing parity for identical CPT-coded services.
  • HB 7191 — Connecticut Children’s pediatric parity (Jim Schmerling) (04:14:33)
    Supports pediatric parity acknowledging higher pediatric care inputs; questions study methodology excluding managed care supplemental payments.
  • HB 7188 — CHESS continuation and redesign (Kathy Zall) (04:18:48)
    Urges learning from implementation challenges, adopting flexible housing outcomes beyond vouchers, and raising hourly rates to attract providers.
  • SB 1471 — Memory care pilot for low-income older adults (CRT) (04:26:43)
    CRT proposes a dedicated secured memory care floor within affordable assisted living to prevent unnecessary nursing facility placement.
  • HB 7191 — Primary care testimony (Dr. Lacey Whitmire) (04:33:18)
    Physician emphasizes moral imperative to fund Medicaid at sustainable levels to protect vulnerable patients and maintain provider workforce.
  • HB 7191 — Anesthesiology rate inadequacy (Dr. John Satterfield) (04:36:54)
    Highlights decades of Medicare underpayment and urges Medicaid increases, stating Medicare-linked rates may still underpay anesthesia services.
  • HB 7191 / SB 1474 — Chiropractic evidence & opioid alternatives (04:40:00)
    Dr. Francis Veschi supports HB 7191 and proposes SB 1474 language to explicitly reference non-opioid therapies, including chiropractic care, for pain management.
  • SB 11 & SB 1251 — Obesity medications (parent perspective) (04:50:27)
    Rick Bachman urges implementing broad obesity medication coverage in SB 11 and opposes SB 1251’s repeal limiting coverage only to Type 2 diabetes.
  • HB 7191 — Podiatry reimbursement shortfalls (Dr. Adam Yosunska) (04:54:49)
    Explains unsustainable Medicaid differentials (e.g., $32 vs. $114 Medicare rate) and access limits due to practice financial stability.
  • HB 7191 — Hospital underpayment and redesign (CHA) (04:58:23)
    CHA notes less than $0.60 per Medicaid dollar, $1.4B shortfall; urges comprehensive redesign using hospital tax and savings reinvestment.
  • HB 7189 — Medication management in residential programs (Continuum of Care) (05:02:24)
    Highlights inconsistencies between DDS and DMHAS programs on medication administration, continuity of care issues, and calls for reforms.
  • HB 7191 — Additional FQHC testimony (Marketing/Client perspectives) (05:05:31)
    Fair Haven CHC staff provide patient and operations perspectives on the necessity of rebasing, COLAs, and rate adequacy.
  • HB 7191 — Patient perspective on GA/Husky (05:08:34)
    A Husky GA enrollee recounts lifesaving prenatal care access and difficulties finding providers who accept Husky due to low rates.
  • HB 7191 — School-Based Health Centers (05:11:37)
    CASBHC emphasizes SBHCs’ role in access to preventive and behavioral health care, workforce challenges, and need for competitive rates.
  • SB 807 / Emergency Medicaid / SB 1251 (DRCT) (05:16:50)
    Disability Rights CT urges faster Husky C asset limit reforms, emergency Medicaid expansion consistent with federal reimbursement, and adherence to obesity med statute.
  • HB 7191 — MEI vs Medicare indexing (CSMS) (05:21:00)
    CSMS supports MEI-based updates, warns against tying to unstable Medicare rates, and calls for broad specialty inclusion to improve access.
  • SB 1251 — MedConnect and obesity meds (beneficiary) (05:34:18)
    MedConnect enrollee opposes eliminating scheduled MedConnect improvements and restricting obesity meds, explains cost-sharing and eligibility impacts.
  • SB 1251 — Domestic violence assistance program realignment (CCADV) (05:38:39)
    CCADV is willing to administer the DV assistance program but seeks adequate funding for both benefits and statewide hotline sustainability.
  • HB 7191 — Behavioral health operations (Fair Haven CHC) (06:38:31)
    Audrey Huang details growing waitlists, under-reimbursed programs, and staff burnout; supports rebasing and predictable increases.
  • SB 1482 / HB 7190 — Senior nutrition and study coordination (Aging services) (06:41:54)
    Aging services representative cautions about administrative burdens in SNAP/meal integration, notes ongoing studies, and coordination across programs.
  • SB 806 / SB 807 — Aging network support (NCAAA) (06:51:23)
    Area Agency on Aging leader supports Husky C asset reforms and emphasizes Medicaid home- and community-based care savings vs. nursing home costs.
  • HB 7191 — Community behavioral health provider needs (CHR) (06:58:23)
    CHR urges immediate rate increases for behavioral health and annual updates, citing staffing shortage and access constraints.
  • SB 807 & HB 7191 — AgingCT support (Marie Allen) (07:12:29)
    Supports asset limit phase-out but urges accelerated timeline; reiterates need for Medicaid rebasing and explores SNAP/Meals policy alignment.
  • SB 11 — PhRMA perspective on CPI cap & 340B (07:17:49)
    PHRMA opposes CPI-referenced price caps and state 340B expansions, arguing net prices are controlled and current 340B growth is misaligned with patient benefits.
  • SB 807 / SB 1251 / HB 7191 — NAMI priorities (07:21:36)
    NAMI CT supports Husky C asset reform and Medicaid rate increases; opposes cutting planned MedConnect improvements; urges addressing income-limit unfairness.
  • HB 7191 — Pediatric dental access (Brooker Memorial) (07:24:31)
    Brooker Memorial details losses due to low dental Medicaid rates and urges parity with FQHC PPS to sustain services for low-income children.
  • HB 7191 — Chiropractic access and parity (Dr. Ali Mendelson) (07:34:25)
    Calls for at least Medicare-level rates for spinal manipulation to expand chiropractic participation and reduce downstream costs.
  • HB 7191 & SB 1474 — Acupuncture and opioid alternatives (07:36:38)
    CT Society of Acupuncturists supports HB 7191 and requests SB 1474 language to reference non-opioid options, including acupuncture, for pain management.
  • SB 1481 — Non-discrimination in healthcare (LGB Courage) (09:00:11)
    Testifier opposes SB 1481 as written, urging protections for evidence-based practice decisions and cautioning against compelled provision of controversial interventions.
  • HB 7192 & SB 11 — Oncology association perspective (09:02:42)
    COA warns against drug importation logistics and PDAB frameworks; urges stronger patient-choice provisions and PBM fiduciary duties aligned to patients.

[Representative Jillian Gilchrist]: Good morning, everyone. Welcome to the Tuesday, March 11, human services committee public hearing. Before we kick it off, any remarks from a good cochair?

[Senator Matt Lesser]: We have a lot of people to hear from today, so I don't wanna take up anything from these time.

[Representative Jillian Gilchrist]: And how about my ranking member, Representative Keith?

[Unidentified ranking member]: Let's get going.

[Representative Jillian Gilchrist]: Alright. First up, we have our state treasurer, Eric Russell, joining us via Zoom.

[State Treasurer Erick Russell]: Morning. Senator Lesser, representative Gilchrist, senator Harding, representative Case, and members of the Human Services Committee, thank you for raising senate bill fourteen seventy six concerning the ABLE Act, and for the opportunity to testify in support of its passage today. I wanna thank this committee for its longstanding commitment to people with disabilities through its support of ABLE CT. As you know, the Office of the Treasury administers ABLE CT, Connecticut's federally qualified program that allows people with disabilities to accumulate savings that can be used to help them with their disabilities without losing their eligibility for federal or state benefits such as Medicaid or supplemental security income. ABLE CT accounts are invested and managed through the National ABLE Alliance, which is a coalition of 19 states, including Connecticut. And this coalition allows us to have strength in numbers by increasing the overall amount of money available to invest on behalf of account owners. This bill updates the ABLE CT statute to align with recent changes in federal law and to ensure future changes to federal law are automatically incorporated by reference. First, it increases the age of diagnosis, which is the determinant for eligibility for an ABLE account and increases that age from 26 to 46 effective 01/01/2026. The change is expected to significantly increase the pool of eligible beneficiaries, including an estimated 1,000,000 military veterans nationwide. So this change has been made to the federal law already, and we need to update the Connecticut ABLE statute to reflect that. And as I mentioned, the bill also provides that the future changes to the ABLE law at the federal level will automatically be incorporated at the state level, so we won't need to go through this exercise every time there is a change on the federal front. Additionally, the bill gives the treasurer the authority to cover account maintenance fees within available appropriations for individual ABLE CT accounts. So while these fees might seem small, they do create a significant burden to ABLE account holders at times because the accounts are often not, substantial. So a 30 or $50 annual fee on an account with just a few $100 in it can be frustrating for, this constituency. Even with investment gains, they often see their balances go up and down, and so, we wanna be able to ease that burden on the program beneficiaries. And we expect that the total cost is is estimated to be less than $50,000 per year. Finally, the bill requires that ABLE CT accounts are disregarded for all means tested programs, not just those currently listed in the statute. This year marks the tenth anniversary of the National ABLE Initiative, and Connecticut joined the program in 2020. It's been incredibly impactful allowing members of the disabled community to make financially responsible decisions and accumulate more money to cover their future needs without worrying about exceeding the assets they are allowed to have under other programs. Prior to ABLE, saving for the future often meant disabled someone living with disabilities would risk losing essential benefits, like Medicaid. So by further safeguarding these protections and expanding them to people who became disabled later in life, including, many wounded veterans, we can provide individuals with greater peace of mind as they save for critical expenses like housing and assistive technology and medical care. Able really is a pathway to independence and opportunity and security for our disabled citizens. And so we're working to expand the impact of ABLE C team so as many people as possible can reap the benefits. And I thank you all for supporting the important enhancements reflected in this bill and for allowing me to testify today. And with that, happy to take any questions that the committee may have.

[Representative Jillian Gilchrist]: Thank you, treasurer Russell, for your work on this important legislation. Representative Case. Oh, senator Lester followed by representative Case.

[Senator Stephen Harding]: No. No. No. You can go first. Okay.

[Representative Jillian Gilchrist]: Representative Case.

[Representative Jay Case]: Here we go. Hey. Thank you, mister Russell, for coming forward. So with this ABLE account, what we're actually helping the IDD and disabled population. In in previous years, most IDD population were forced to go out and do special needs trusts. But this allows people in the disability population to save. What are they actually able to spend this on down the line through you?

[State Treasurer Erick Russell]: Sure. So thank you for the question. So they that's pretty flexible. It's anything really that's related to living expenses or medical expenses. It's pretty broad. I would say that in addition to the benefits of the kind of savings without a jeopardizing programs, there's a tax deduction benefit much like our five twenty nine college savings programs where up to $10,000 can be deducted as it's being contributed to these accounts, and they're getting the benefit of the fact that these monies are invested after they are put into into the the ABLE accounts as well.

[Representative Jay Case]: So and and I think the key point of this, as you stated, it doesn't require the downspend in order for a disabled person to stay on state or federal benefits. And I think the downspend would be in Connecticut to 1,600 level. And I think it's imperative that people who are able to and that's why it's a able act to save to because we do have more expenses on the personal level throughout our lives, so they're disabled or you're not. So this is one way to move forward. I appreciate your office taking this on and allowing for those accounts to grow so that people can actually have a better way to fund their life. Thank you.

[State Treasurer Erick Russell]: Thank you.

[Representative Jillian Gilchrist]: Senator Lesser.

[Senator Matt Lesser]: Thank you, madam chair, and thank you, miss treasurer. It's good to see you, and appreciate your support of ABLE, which is a fantastic program. Just understanding what this bill does, though, it does something that we don't we're not doing a ton of these days, which is adopting by reference federal laws and regulations, which, of course, can change from time to time. Is there a specific change that we're anticipating? And, you know, how do you obviously, at other times, we may wanna decouple. So how are you looking potential changes if there's or is this just a general policy that will hopefully keep us more harmonized with with the federal regs?

[State Treasurer Erick Russell]: So great question. Thank you, Senator. So first, there is the change that is coming up on January 1 as is outlined in the legislation, which is which is the expansion of eligibility from the age of 26 to 46. You know, we are part of, as I mentioned, this national ABLE alliance and much of the work that's happening to move ABLE forward is really happening on this collaborative basis. And so there's generally really broad support as initiatives are moving forward on on the federal level as well. The thought here was that, you know, putting something in statute so that some of these small tweaks and changes that may be made over time to the program wouldn't require us to come back to the legislature every time. Past, you know, the the age adjustment, I don't know of any immediate anticipated changes to the program, but we wanted to be proactive and so that we're aligned with what's happening more broadly.

[Senator Matt Lesser]: Great. Thank you very much.

[Representative Jillian Gilchrist]: Great. Thank you. Any other question? Alright. Seeing no hands, thank you so much for being with us today.

[State Treasurer Erick Russell]: Thank you very much.

[Representative Jillian Gilchrist]: Next, we'll hear from commissioner of the Department of Social Services, Andrea Barton Reeves.

[Commissioner Andrea Barton Reeves]: Good morning.

[Representative Jillian Gilchrist]: Good morning.

[Commissioner Andrea Barton Reeves]: Good morning. Good morning, Chairs Lester and Gilchrist and ranking members, Harding and case and distinguished members of the human services committee. I am Andrea Barton Lees, the commissioner for the Department of social services and I am. Pleased to be here today to offer some remarks on several of the bills on today's agenda. I'll note upfront that two things if you don't mind we have a lot of bills. I will not be testifying on all of them today. I just want everybody to be released by that. I have a few that I just like to bring to your attention, and not even in their entirety. So I will not have you here forever. And the second is that, as I am testifying, there will be instances where I will raise our opposition or concern to a bill, and that might be because of fiscal policy or other concerns that we have. But that is not an indication that we are not open to having any further dialogue and on any of the policy issues that are raised in the bills. We're happy to continue to have that conversation with you. So thank you. So I will start with Senate Bill fourteen eighty two and act concerning elderly nutrition. So the other thing I want to mention is they do have a few subject matter experts here with me if we have additional questions. Thank you. So as we know, this bill requires that DSS in consultation with the commissioner of aging and disabilities maximize the use of federal SNAP or Supplemental Nutrition Assistance Program funds for nutritious meals for elderly, persons. What we know now is that SNAP does support over 479,000 people, and of that number, eight hundred eighty eight thousand three hundred are over the age of 60. Right now, we do we do this under a a waiver that's known as the Elderly Simplified Application Project, which we know internally as ESAP, and that allows us to increase participation among older adults who are low income, who are facing barriers to participation by allowing them to extend their benefit period. So to increase awareness of the SNAP program, there was legislation that was passed in October 2024, and we have been meeting with the trade association, CAPCA, of the community action agencies to find ways that we can expand SNAP. So as a result of that, there was more SNAP outreach last November, and we continue to have stakeholder meetings. Finally, the federal regulations already allow individuals age 60 plus to access prepared meals under SNAP through the Meals on Wheels program, which is also administered through our community action agencies. So that does allow for those who are age 60 and older to get, wheel Meals on Wheels as well as what we know what is known as communal dining facilities. And, the United States Department of Agriculture's Food and Nutrition Services, which we colloquially refer to as FNS, is the only entity that can authorize retailers, including Meals on Wheels and Communal Dyna Nineties to participate in the program. So at at this stage, there are a number of programs in which we are trying to maximize the use of SNAP benefits, particularly for our older population. And so we believe that we are taking appropriate measures, but certainly open to more discussion as to how we can, better maximize these resources. Next, I'll go to Senate Bill fourteen seventy five, again, concerning food deserts and the SNAP program. This bill would require DSS to develop and implement a plan to participate in what's known as the restaurant meals program, typically known as RNP. It is, in fact, a state option that some states have have engaged in that serves members who are homeless and older adults 60 and above are disabled to use their SNAP benefits to purchase meals at participating restaurants. And each state can, in fact, set its own standard for that, but the restaurants have to agree to participate under certain conditions. And those include offering low cost meals at a discount. Those meals have to cost less than what would normally be charged. Customers who don't use SNAP, and those meals have to be defined as meals that are ready. They're offered to certain customers, and they cannot charge a gratuity or a sales tax. We do estimate that there would be a significant lift with respect to implementing this program, but we're we're also finding, and I'll probably be testifying to that a little bit later in a different section, is that there is very little take up of this in other states. And where it is taken up, it's primarily in fast food restaurants, which that where SNAP benefits could be used. So currently, there are only nine states that use the RNP program on a very limited basis. In Rhode Island, which is one that's really closest to us and probably the the most, the state that has the greatest comparison in terms of our geographic location and the number of people that live in the state, that they only have eight Subway restaurants in two counties that are actually choosing to participate. And in New York, which we know is a different population, there's also fairly low take up. That's about 104 restaurants, and 72% of those are considered fast food chains. There is a larger one. That one is in Arizona, and you can see that in my written testimony. That has about eight sixty six, and 600 of those are fast food establishments. There are a number of steps that we'd have to take, which are outlined in the testimony that we would have to do in order to be able to provide, a restaurant meal program in the state, and we'd have to constantly monitor it. At this moment, the department is not set up to actually administer this program, But it does raise a very critical question. And that is if you don't use the restaurant meal program, and this is the policy question that we'd really love to have a conversation with with the committee, then how can we support those who want access to meals, who are staff recipients, particularly those that are unhoused, where access would really be very important. We, you know, we have these discussions. I know sometimes it's hard for people to believe this, but we actually do have these policy discussions very seriously at DSS. And we were thinking, well, are there other ways that we could do this? But we don't wanna make that decision or have that discussion unilaterally. So we appreciate the bill and actually would like to use it as an opportunity to continue to have more discussion about the ways that we can help people, particularly people who are unhoused to get access to food on a on a regular basis. And so that is my testimony regarding that particular bill. Then I'd like to move to Senate Bill fourteen seventy one, which is an act concerning a pilot program, to provide community based memory care, programs. We would very much like to work with the community to to build a pilot program. The challenge with the statute as it's written is that we cannot have a a targeted catchment area under Medicaid. We can have sometimes targeted catchment areas and even targeted populations that we serve under a waiver, but we can't do it under the auspices of the program itself. That's that's prohibited. So, however, we know that the need for low cost housing and affordable housing for elders is very important. So what we'd like to do is if if, the committee is amenable, we'd like to continue to have some conversation so that we could figure out a way that we could do this that might include, ways in which Medicaid could support it. But as it's currently drafted, we couldn't do that because of what I just mentioned. But I we would love to have more discussion about that and perhaps also bring in our partners from the Department of Housing as well. Next, I will go to Senate Bill 11, which we know is a very significant bill, and I'm only gonna highlight certain parts of that because you do have my full testimony in front of you. I'd like to speak specifically to section seven, if you don't mind, and that is the section that requires nursing home facilities to spend at least 80% of their Medicaid, Medicare, and all other forms of patient revenue on direct care costs. There are some challenges that we'd like to raise respect with respect to this bill. The first is that in 2022, when the thank you to the legislature. We were able, with your support, to incorporate what we call an acuity based system. So this really does allow us to recognize the importance of having a continuity of care, aligning the Medicaid payments with the acuity of a facility's particular residents and providing what we call value based payment approaches to increased care. The challenge that we're having with the statute as it's currently written is that care in Medicaid for nursing homes falls into four categories. They are direct, they are indirect, they're capital and administrative, sorry, five, and property or fair rent. In order for us to redirect all of those resources primarily to patient care, that would then require several things, one of which is that we would have to have every nursing home and long term care facility actually segregate those dollars, so that we could track them dollar by dollar. And then it would it would deprive them of the flexibility that they need to use the Medicaid dollars in the areas where it's needed to maintain the facility, to pay staff, to all of the things that they need to do to create a more holistic, healthy, area and facility for where elders can stay. So if we devoted 80% of the Medicaid costs toward direct care, then that would only leave 20% for the other things like dietary staff, food, laundry, resident supplies, all of the things that really contribute to the overall health, safety, and well-being, including paying taxes and rent and and the like, in nursing homes. So what we were suggesting here is that as it's currently written, and forgive us if we're being presumptuous, we think that the idea is that we want to make sure that there's greater accountability as to how Medicaid dollars are being used in the nursing homes, and there are there are other ways that we can account for that. We do have nursing homes, and I do have our our nursing home finance expert with me today. So she may be able to answer some questions about that, where they do provide cost reports, and we can see what it is that they are paying. So perhaps maybe digging into that a little more deeply might provide some more, transparency and clarity. Next is section eight that I'd like to speak to that requires, the establishment of a new definition of emergency medical condition. We do appreciate having this section, in this in the proposed statute, but there are some parts of it that don't align with what don't align with federal law, and that limits our ability to obtain, Medicaid reimbursement for some of the things that we do. So it there is right now a definition of what is considered a limited medical emergency, and that is specifically an emergency that occurs that requires treatment that is that is required after what is, quote, the sudden onset of a medical condition manifesting itself by acute acute symptoms of sufficient severity, including severe pain. So I'm reading directly from the statute. And there are other criteria there, including that it places the patient's health in jeopardy. It it causes a serious impairment to their bodily functions, or it's a serious dysfunction of antibody or organ. There are a wide range of conditions and diagnoses that are already covered under emergency Medicaid because it's required for us to do that under the statute and not by a specific diagnosis. So our position here is that as the as the Medicaid rules already provide for, there is significant, coverage that's provided as emergency Medicaid, coverage is already defined within the Medicaid statute. And as we see there, we have not been able to, find where those are not covered. [Yvette Highsmith]: Good afternoon, to excuse me for a second. Thank you to senator Lesser and representative Gilchrist and the distinguished members of the human services committee. I am Yvette Highsmith, vice president of the Eastern Region at Community Health Center Inc. And I'm speaking today in support of raised House Bill number 7,191, in acts concerning Medicaid rate increases, planning and sustainability. Community Health Center Inc is the state's largest federally qualified health center. And we've been providing healthcare to generations of families in our state since 1972. Currently, we care for more than 110,000 patients at our locations throughout the state. And that includes brick and mortar primary care centers, shelters, and our robust network of school based care locations. We're a critical component of the state's healthcare system, providing medical, dental, and behavioral health services for all ages in every corner of Connecticut, regardless of ability to pay. About 60% of our patients are Medicaid members. Now for nearly twenty years though, the state has failed to live up to its obligation to provide cost based reimbursement for Medicaid medical visits. And the shortfall is significant. As CHC, we currently lose $110 on each of these visits. We are in effect subsidizing the care of Medicaid medical patients, diverting resources, securing grants and donor funds in order to compensate for this state created deficit. And this was never how the system was designed to operate. The intent of the original legislation creating federally qualified health centers was for these monies to support the needs of uninsured and underinsured patients. With the potentially devastating funding cuts coming to Medicaid and the federal government, these losses are unsustainable. So we enthusiastically support House Bill 7,191 in accordance with the recent rate study commissioned by the Department of Social Services as we heard from the Commissioner. This bill rights the wrongs of the past and puts FQHCs on the path to financial sustainability. It lays out a clear process and timeline by which DSS will review and rebase rates that better reflect the true cost of care and phased in over time so as not to unduly burden the state's finances. Without the clear and reasonable approach set forth in this House Bill, federally qualified health centers will be unable to fulfill their mandate of delivering quality care for all, keeping those most in need healthy and productive residents of our state. We're already seeing some cutbacks and layoffs that are happening at our health centers, and the future would require further reductions in order to access care if we stay on the current path. I urge your support for this important and timely legislation, and thank you for your consideration. Also happy to answer any questions.

[Representative Jillian Gilchrist]: Thank you so much. Thank you for your work and for your testimony. I don't

[Commissioner Deidre Gifford]: Good morning. Sorry. Good afternoon. Senator Lesser, representative Gilchrist, senator Harding, representative Case. I'm Diedre Gifford, the commissioner from the Office of Health Strategy. It's very nice to be back with the Human Services Committee. I have just brief testimony this morning on a few of the bills before you. First of all, beginning with senate bill 11 and house bill seventy one ninety two, thank you for the opportunity to testify on these important bills that address prescription drug affordability. As we all know, prescription drug prices have increased substantially in the last decade. We report on this in our, health care benchmark initiative annual report. We've we know that those who struggle the most with rising prescription drug costs are those on multiple medications, those who are younger, those with lower incomes. And we've seen price increases of 15% per year on drugs when which can amount to $590 per drug product, so a real challenge. The Office of Health Strategy benchmark report found that PMPM commercial per member per month commercial spending has gone up by as much as 14% per year. So we appreciate and support senate bill 11 and house bill seventy one ninety two, which both take important steps to address these prescription drug cost increases, including studying the feasibility of establishing Canadian drug importation and also limiting price growth for generic and off patent prescriptions. These initiatives align with proposals made by the governor and h b sixty eight seventy, which would also limit price increases for generic and off patent drugs to the annual rate of inflation and establish a pathway for safe Canadian prescription drug importation after an assessment by DCP. OHS had a look at the, impact of the generic prescription drug increase, proposal that would we identified more than 400 generic prescription drugs that would have had their wholesale price limited by the governor's proposal. And we also we also looked at off patent and biologics, and our initial estimates are that had the governors and these proposals been in place, we would have seen reductions in wholesale costs by about $9,000,000 per year. We also note that, both bills, allude to provisions, in the pharmaceutical benefit manager report that the legislature asked OHS to produce and that we transmitted to the committee and the prescription drug task force. Regarding senate bill fourteen seventy seven, an act concerning a covered Connecticut trust fund. OHS appreciates the intent of this legislation. As you know, covered Connecticut was conceived to reduce further our state's uninsured rate and make coverage affordable. The program, as you know, offers no cost health insurance, dental insurance, and nonemergency medical transportation to qualifying individuals be with incomes between 138 and a 175% of the federal poverty level. We're very proud that as of January, the program has now enrolled 42,000 individuals. Although the program is administered, through DSS, OHS has had a role in the development implementation, oversight, and doing outreach for Covered Connecticut. This bill would establish a trust fund with the purpose of promoting sustainability of the Covered Connecticut pro Connecticut program and would require OHS in consultation with CID and DSS to write a strategic plan on how to use the funds. Unfortunately, the creation of a non lapsing fund for dedicated purposes would further limit the state's ability flexibility to implement other programs within available resources. It would also bind the hands of future administrations and legislatures to balance competing needs. Since the funds were not required funds required were not included in the governor's budget, the agency does not support the bill as written. And finally, regarding senate bill fourteen eighty, an act concerning private equity and real estate investment trust, ownership of hospitals and nursing homes. We also appreciate the intent of this legislation, which which seeks to limit new ownership interest by private equity or real estate investment trusts in hospitals or nursing home facilities that receive Husky Medicaid reimbursement for services. We strongly support the transparency and review of health care transactions and believe that these are important tools for addressing consolidation and financialization of health care. As you know, to address this rapidly shifting landscape in the health care industry, the governor has also proposed house bill sixty eight seventy three, which would cast a wide net to ensure that the full spectrum of health care transactions receive review, transactions that currently do not, such as those involved with private equity and real estate investment trusts. The governor's bill would allow the office of the attorney general to place conditions on transactions that have been identified as having the potential to do harm, and it also gives the state regulatory oversight without an outright prohibition on any particular type of investment strategy or vehicle. OHS shares the committee's interest in further monitoring and, where appropriate, placing conditions on transactions that have the potential to cause financial instability and lower the quality of health care, and we look forward to working with the committee to explore how the governor's proposals and those of SB fourteen eighty can be harmonized to address the challenges that SB fourteen eighty seems to solve, aims to solve. That concludes my testimony. Thank you very much, and I'm happy to take any questions.

[Representative Jillian Gilchrist]: Wonderful. Thank you so much, commissioner, for being here. You're welcome. [Commissioner Deidre Gifford]: Happy to. So first of all, I would start with the health care benchmark initiative, which is charged with not only measuring and monitoring health care cost increases year over year, but also identifying the drivers of those increased costs. Consistently, since the benchmark program was put into place, what we've seen is that drug prices, particularly retail pharmacy, as a key driver of health care cost increases year over year. The all payer claims database, which is housed at the office of health strategy, has been used to look at some of the details underneath these increases in retail pharmacy prices. That's some some of the data that I cited in my testimony has allowed us to look at specific drugs. But what we've learned through our hearings and dealing with stakeholders is that it's very challenging to understand, drug price increases because of the interplay between all the different, parts of that very, very complex financing system, and PBMs are a a very significant part of that. You all charged us last year with doing a study of PBM practices. We transmitted that to to you all a a month or two ago, and several of the recommendations in these bills align with some of the recommendations that were highlighted in that study. In particular, the fiduciary duty of PBMs was highlighted in that study and prohibiting spread pricing arrangements and just in general, giving CID more ability to have transparency into the activities of PBMs.

I'm here to offer comments on three bills being heard today, senate bill twelve fifty one, senate bill fourteen eighty two, and house bill seven one nine o. So section one of senate bill twelve fifty one, the health and human services implementer transfers the administration of our driver training program for people with disabilities from ADS back to the Department of Motor Vehicles, as well as transferring the program's budget. This program provides free driver training to individuals with disabilities who require adaptive equipment to drive, and it was originally housed within within DMV for about thirty five years before it was transferred to our agency back in 2011. The ultimate goal of the transfer when we're when I've been talking with Commissioner Guerrera and the teams from our agencies, the ultimate goal is guaranteeing the same access for people with disabilities to licensing services that people with without disabilities have. So right now, individuals who need special specialized equipment need to come to our agency. It's an extra step in the process for somebody who has a disability. And so we wanna make sure that DMV has that full access for individuals with disabilities, and we can support them. We have three tremendous staff. It's not a really big program. We we serve about 300 people, just under 300 people a year, and we have three tremendous, employees who provide the services to folks. Bringing their expertise back to DMV not only continues to provide the special specialized, training that folks need, but it also helps DMV expand their reach, be able to make sure that folks who don't need training but have special equipment, maybe somebody who comes from another state, can have immediate access to licensing services. So we believe that this transfer, is better aligns the services, better aligns the training curriculum with our road readiness driver training. The second bill, senate bill fourteen eighty two, would require our agency to consult with d s s to maximize the use of federal snap funds. [Kathleen Holt]: Thank you. Representative Gilchrist, senator Lesser, representative Case, Senator Harding, and members of the Human Services Committee. I am Kathleen Holt, health care advocate for the state of Connecticut. I'll focus on sections sixteen and seventeen of Senate Bill twelve fifty one, an act implementing the governor's recommendations. I'm concerned this could fundamentally change the consumer focused mission of the office of the health care advocate, OHA. For the past twenty five years, OHA has diligently served all the health care insurance needs of consumers in Connecticut, including for behavioral health, mental health, and physical health. We never discriminate between helping someone with one type of health care issue or another. We address all requests equally. So if I am de facto behavioral health care advocate as the health care advocate, people have asked me why do we need another one. It's confusing. The clarifying title for this role as it was envisioned should be the behavioral health care provider advocate. The office was created in 2023 by public act twenty three one zero one with a provider focused mission to help mental health and behavioral health providers enroll in insurance plans and navigate provider issues. That office is not yet operational, but an advisory committee has been formed made up of six members who are all providers. It would be a mistake to intertwine OHA's consumer advocacy mission with a provider focused mission. While helping providers may ultimately promote service access for consumers, provider and consumer interests do not always align. The elimination of a clear boundary separating the two offices could lead to confusion and conflict as to which party, provider or consumer, is being supported in a given case. It is critical to OHA's mission that our independent agency remains consumer focused, trusted by consumers, acting on behalf of consumers, and speaking out from the consumer perspective. This is what makes our office unique from other state agencies and counted on by consumers. Our office and our advisory committee consists of individuals fully focused on the consumer experience. Providers and insurers have associations and lobbyists. Consumers have OHA. Ultimately, if OHA is to absorb the office of the behavioral health care provider advocate, there would need to be a clear firewall between the work for and on behalf of consumers and for and on behalf of providers. Otherwise, if not implemented properly, it will jeopardize OHA's mission and reputation and unfairly be disruptive for consumers and to the strong reputation OHA has cultivated as a highly competent and successful voice for health care consumers. [Mairead Painter]: Good morning, senator or afternoon, senator Lesser, representative Gilchrist, senator Harding, representative Case, and distinguished members of the Human Services Committee. I'm Marie Painter, the state long term care ombudsman, and I'll be testifying on several bills today. The first one being s b eight zero five, an act concerning nursing homes, to spend not less than 80% of revenue on direct patient care. While I appreciate the intent of this legislation and fully support a defined percentage of how Medicaid and other public dollars are allocated, it's important to consider how direct care is defined here in Connecticut. Compared to other states, Connecticut has a very narrow definition of direct care And it doesn't include several critical support services like laundry, housekeeping, social work, and meal services that are all essential components of high quality care, but not included in the definition of direct care. Other states have higher requirements as far as percentages going to direct care. However, they have much broader definitions of direct care. I believe it's important to keep this narrow definition in our state and then define what is indirect care and capital expenses, and the requirements around them. Having a clear understanding of where all public dollars are being spent. It is important to have this clear understanding where public dollars are going. This will improve the quality of care, provide higher wages for staff, and ensure that necessary improvements to physical plant are done. Currently, we have buildings where they have received funds year over year, yet little to no improvements have been made to the general maintenance of these buildings. This can lead to evacuations and closures, ensuring that nursing homes and all of their public dollars are used to enhance the care and services for residents is our goal. I support efforts to create greater transparency and accountability in how these funds are spent. However, requirements must take into effect our account the full range of services and necessary expenses to ensure only high quality of care in our nursing homes. The next bill is SB fourteen seventy nine, an act concerning arbitration and health care. This bill provides safeguards to residents of Medicaid recipients, particularly in nursing homes, by prohibiting healthcare providers from requiring mandatory arbitration for billing disputes as conditions of Medicaid reimbursement. Mandatory arbitration clauses are often embedded in complex agreements that residents and their families may not fully understand. These clauses strip individuals of their right to seek legal counsel in court and instead forces them into private arbitration systems that overwhelmingly favor providers. This bill ensures that Medicaid recipients retain access to the judicial system and can contest unfair billing practices. The next bill is SB1480, an act concerning private equity and real estate investment trust ownership of hospitals and nursing homes. Private equity firms and large real estate investment trusts have increasingly acquired acquired nursing homes, often prioritizing profits over resident care. The business models of these entities frequently includes aggressive cost cutting strategies, staffing reduction, and focus on short term financial returns, all of which negatively affect the quality of care, staffing levels, and resident outcomes. Evidence from multiple studies that I referenced in my written testimony demonstrate the harm that private equity and real estate trust owner facilities inflict on nursing home residents. For example, research published by the JAM Health Foundation found that private equity ownership of nursing homes led to a ten percent increase in short term mortality rates among residents. Over a twelve year period, this translated to more than twenty thousand additional resident deaths. Additionally, residents in PE acquired facilities were eleven percent more likely to visit emergency departments and eight point seven percent more likely to be hospitalized for avoidable conditions, highlighting a concern of declining quality of care. Staffing reductions are another major concern that is a consequence of private equity ownership, as these homes saw a 3% decline in nursing staff hours per resident day with the greatest reduction occurring among registered nurses. Given that RN staffing is a key predictor in positive health outcomes, these reductions present serious risks to residents. A study by the Center of Medicare Advocacy, Tony Edelman, found that PE backed nursing homes were more likely to have higher numbers of deficiencies and violations, including inadequate infection control and insufficient direct care staff. Financial strategies employed by private equity firms further divert the resources away from resident care. These firms often engage in sale leaseback agreements, selling the nursing home properties and leasing them back at higher rates, which drains financial resources from operational and care expenses. Funds that could have been reinvested into direct care and instead funneled into management fees, dividends, and debt servicing. One analysis found that private equity firms extracted $1,300,000,000 in profits from a major nursing home chain while simultaneously facing staffing shortages and declines in resident outcomes. The lack of transparency and ownership structures further exacerbates this problem. By prohibiting private equity firms and real estate trusts from acquiring and increasing ownership and control, this bill helps protect residents from the instability, financial restructuring, and profit driven decision making that often accompany these ownership structures. I provided, as I said, all of the links in my testimony to this research and the findings I referenced. I appreciate your time and I'm available for any questions.

At Fairhaven, we recently expanded our Brantford location, due to growing patient needs, adding five clinical rooms and additional pro and additional providers. Despite clear evidence of increased service demand, our request for a Medicaid rate increase was denied. This broken and inconsistent process puts health care centers in an impassable possession, forced to stretch already limited resources to serve patients who depend on us. And as we know from two Federal Trade Commission reports, including one that came out in January, there's only a few big PBMs in this country. They account for over 80% of dispensing of these drugs. They make billions of dollars off of this. They do certainly generate a lot of savings for themselves. We need to pass that down and make certain also that there's a fair deal for the local pharmacies that our constituents rely on. And I know the FDA every year issues an updated report and the list of drug shortages increases every year. I see it every day in my practice. What can we do with importation of drugs from Canada, which is allowed if the state does seek that? Florida got permission to do that, the first state in the nation. And, SB 11 introduces key provisions to address these concerns in, including, the key one of the establishment of a fiduciary duty for PBMs to act in the best interest of consumers and health plans. And SB 11 continues the momentum of previous efforts, where a few years ago we, capped the out of pocket costs for insulin and also prohibited PBMs from imposing gag orders on pharmacists to prevent customers from saving money on certain lower cost generics. And the, the bill addresses this affordability crisis by expanding state agencies, ability to negotiate, bulk purchases of drugs and also strengthens collective bargaining power to drive down costs for, public programs and taxpayers. And, reinforces Connecticut's commitment to fair pricing and also strengthens protections for the three forty B, contract pharmacies, ensuring that discounted drugs intended for vulnerable populations are not undermined, by predatory PBMs or, or, or manufacturers, and their practices. And also the bill establishes a framework, to limit excessive pricing, on prescription drugs, and tackles the problem of anti competitive practices by addressing pay to delay agreements, wherein pharmaceutical manufacturers intentionally delay the release of lower cost generics, to maintain high prices artificially. Also introduces a Canadian drug importation program, to lower prescription drug costs and enhance, market competition. All of these are key elements of this far reaching bill, high priority for our our caucus, and we'll turn it over to our majority leader.

[Kathleen Garrity]: Thank you, Senator Lesser, Representative Gilchrist, and members of the Human Service Committee. I am Kathleen Gary, executive director of the Connecticut State Dental Association, and I am privileged to represent the oral health care providers in our state. I'm here today to express our strong support of SB 7,191. I appreciate the committee's continued work to address Medicaid reimbursement rates and pursuing the goal of ensuring adequate and sustainable rates for healthcare providers, including dentists. As I have done often when I appear before you, I would underscore to the committee again today the unique challenges involved in setting dental Medicaid rates. Since most dental procedure codes are not covered by Medicare, it is unreliable for as a reference point for dental reimbursement. While I appreciate the effort to compare Connecticut to peer states such as Maine, Massachusetts, New Jersey, New York, and Oregon to determine, rate increases, I would like to emphasize that each of these states use different methods to set dental rates, methods that are often shaped by their own state budget, cost structures, and policy decisions that do not necessarily reflect the realities of providing care in Connecticut. A far more accurate and sustainable approach for dental rates would be to use Fair Health, a nationally recognized independent database that provides real claims data, including dental procedures. Fair Health data can be broken down by zip code, allowing Connecticut to set rates that reflect the true cost of care in different parts of our state. This ensures rates different parts of our state. This ensures rates are based on what dental providers actually are going to be paid, not what neighboring states decide to pay under unrelated systems. This issue is particularly urgent because dental providers across Connecticut are seeing an increase in cavities and dendritic disease, particularly among children ages six to 11. Without fair reimbursement rates, fewer dental providers will participate in the program, further limiting access to care for the families who need it most. We are already seeing this impact at community health centers, which are reducing dental services due to low Medicaid reimbursement rates. For example, community health and wellness center ceased dental care at the February, citing a $350,000 loss of major procedures last year. Also, Stay Well Health Center has halted costly procedures like crowns and root canals, which are necessary for oral health. These cuts affect over 400,000 residents who rely on these centers, with more than 60% of those being covered by Medicaid. Using Fair Health to set dental Medicaid rates would also create a predictable data driven process for updating rates in the future. It removes the uncertainty that comes from trying to compare to other states whose systems and policies may change unpredictably. Fair health provides a stable, transparent, and objective method to ensure rates keep pace with the actual cost of providing care. I encourage the committee to support race bill 7,191 with the recommendation that Fair Health data be the primary source for determining dental Medicaid rates. This change will strengthen the sustainability of Connecticut's Medicaid dental program while protecting access to care for vulnerable patients. Lastly, we are increasingly concerned that the imposition of tariffs on vital dental supplies coming

[Jim Schmerling]: Senator Lesser, representative Gilchrist, members of the human service committee, thank you for the opportunity to share my thoughts on House Bill 7,191. My name is Jim Schmerling and I'm the President and CEO of Connecticut Children's. We're grateful that the committee raised this bill because the status quo is unsustainable. We're experiencing a financial crisis that is precipitated by Medicaid underpayment and I detailed our need for Medicaid parity in testimony that I submitted last month. We're proposing this change because Medicaid rate parity is not the same as payment to cost parity cost ratio parity. Caring for kids in a hospital setting requires more staff, more time, more services given the size and age range of our patients. All of these additional inputs result in more cost which is why paying the same rates does not lead to parity in the percentage of costs that are actually covered. In fact, given the higher costs in pediatric medicine, paying pediatric providers the same rates as adult providers could result in pediatric Medicaid payment cost ratios getting even worse. Although pediatrics is generally more expensive than adult medicine, Connecticut Children's is not expensive compared to our children's hospital peers. In a report published by Goldman Sachs last year, Connecticut Children's cost per adjusted discharge is the fifth lowest out of 38 children's hospitals and 22% less than the median. Now some may say that Connecticut Children's does not need improved Medicaid rates because our base rate for inpatient care is already one of the highest in the state. This statement is misleading and neglects the fact that the base rate is not inclusive of costs associated with services provided by our medical staff, which are actually paid by the hospital. I would like to share some thoughts with you about the Medicaid rate study and its applicability to pediatrics. This rate study proposes a five rate a five state rate benchmark for services that do not have corresponding Medicare rates but we don't believe that this offers a meaningful metric for comparing pediatric payments. While the study addressed the hospital payments for pediatric stays in children's hospitals, the report appears to have only analyzed published fee for service rates. As I noted in my submitted testimony, most children who live in the study's comparison states participate in managed care arrangements. Providers can often negotiate higher rates and supplemental payments with managed care plans so the study findings likely do not offer a true comparison of the total payments that pediatric hospital providers receive. Connecticut children's Medicaid results lag lag those of the average children's hospital nationally as that discrepancy grows significantly when supplemental payments are included. In 2024 Connecticut children's Medicaid system results showed that we received less than 60¢ for every dollar that we spend on care. The average children's hospital received more than 82¢ with all supplemental payments included. Since the study analysis excluded supplemental payments the benchmark approach will understate total payments and the comparison states, and thus it cannot effectively assess the adequacy of pediatric hospital payments.

With our CHESS staff's help, they were able to secure at least some income and are housed in shared housing with family or friends or in setting like sober houses. Clearly anyone in this 40% would prefer a voucher that allowed them to access their own apartment. But like so many other very low income households, they are finding housing options that provide a stepping stone to better housing as their income increases. Given the reality, the addressing the affordable housing crisis will take a significant amount of time, couldn't it make sense to consider redesigning CHESS requirements to recognize that for many, their next housing option may not be their own apartment, but some other type of housing that is a cost effective stepping stone.

The Medicare system is broken for us. In the process of developing the Medicare payment methodology almost forty years ago, a tragic calculation error was made, and it is now well recognized that anesthesia services are woefully underpaid. Physician specialties typically experience Medicare payment rates that are about 20% lower than their average commercial rates. In 2007 and again in 2020, the government accounting office, the GAO, found that anesthesia payment rates to anesthesiologists from Medicare were 70% lower than commercial rates. And today, that number is actually closer to 75% below commercial rates. Even an adjustment to 300% of Medicare rates for anesthesia services would represent a significant underpayment, one that is well below the cost of providing anesthesia care. Furthermore, the AMA has identified that when adjusted for practice costs from 2000 through 2025, the real value of Medicare payment to all physicians has decreased by 33%. I use all this information to highlight the fact that underpayment by Medicare to anesthesiologists only compounded by current Medicaid reimbursement rates in Connecticut. [Dr. Francis Veschi]: Good afternoon, senator Wessler and representative Gilchrist. I'm doctor Francis Veschi, a chiropractic physician practicing in New Britain, and I'm representing the Connecticut Chiropractic Association today to comment on House Bill 7,191, an act concerning Medicaid rate increases, planning, and sustainability. The chiropractic physician treating a Medicaid Husky patient will typically bill the CPT code corresponding to a one to two area spinal manipulation or a three to four region spinal manipulation. These codes are reimbursed by Medicare at $23.14 and $35.60 respectively, while Medicaid Husky reimbursement is $15.12 and $20.72 respectively. As a result of this low reimbursement, chiropractic physicians in the state are reluctant to become participating physicians in the Medicaid Husky program or have disenrolled from the program altogether. We appreciate that as drafted, House Bill 7,191 will phase in an increase in Medicaid reimbursements over three years up to seventy five percent of the Medicare rate. The CCA would also like to comment on section three of Senate Bill fourteen seventy four, an act concerning the Department of Social Services. This provision will have Medicaid prescribers become familiar with alternative to opioid drugs in the treatment of a patient's pain. We support this change. In August 2022 study published by Canadian researchers in the Journal of Manipulative Therapeutics found that patients with non cancerous spinal pain who underwent chiropractic care were less likely to obtain a prescription for opioids than those who did not. Additionally, a 2020 study published in Pain Medicine showed that patients with spinal pain who saw a chiropractic physician had half the risk of filing an opioid prescription. Further, if the patient sought chiropractic care within thirty days of diagnosis, the risk reduction was greater as compared to those with their first visit after the acute phase. Right now, the role of the the role of chiropractic in providing non pharmacologic strategies for pain reduction is recognized in section twenty-14s of the general statutes. We believe additional language in section three will make it more effective. Our suggestion as follows, new text beginning on line 63. Prescribers may consider the options for referring or prescribing appropriate non opioid treatment alternatives that shall include, but are not limited to, medications, restorative therapies performed by a chiropractic physician, or other appropriate medical professionals. [Mark Schafer]: Good afternoon, representative Gilchrist, senator Lesser, representative Case, senator Perillo, and members of the committee. My name is Mark Schafer, vice president, system innovation and financing at the Connecticut Hospital Association. I appreciate the opportunity to testify concerning house bill 7,191, an act concerning Medicaid rates, rate increases, planning, and sustainability. CHA supports the intent of this bill, which is to address the significant Medicaid underfunding across the health care continuum. And as we've previously testified, Connecticut substantially under invests in Medicaid relative to other Northeastern states. We would spend $2,500,000,000 more a year on Medicaid if our spend was in line with these other states. This is consistent with the findings, of the, recent DSS reimbursement studies. However, we think it's important to note that the phase two study failed to document the substantial hospital underpayment that exists in Medicaid today. Connecticut hospitals are reimbursed less than 60¢ on the dollar for the services provided to Medicaid beneficiaries after accounting for the taxes they pay to fund the state share of Medicaid services. The hospital underpayment has grown sharply in recent years reaching $1,400,000,000 in fiscal year twenty twenty three. According to a recent office of the health strategy report, the Medicaid hospital shortfall in Connecticut is 79% higher than the national average. It's critically important to address this issue. We strongly encourage the legislature to include hospital reimbursement as part of this proposal. We believe that such increases could be enabled by a comprehensive redesign of the Medicaid program, one that could include a better use of Connecticut's hospital tax to advance the important public policy goals that the legislature set forth in section 17 of public act 2,371. These goals included addressing social, economic, and environmental drivers of health, improving the long term health and well-being of the Medicaid population, and promoting affordability for all. There are two elements that are critical to this redesign. The, first, the hospital tax in conjunction with other sources of revenue should be used to reduce or eliminate the Medicaid hospital shortfall and provide for annual updates. And the distribution of these payments should be codified in statute. Second, we believe that a new payment model is needed in Medicaid, one that capitalizes on the role that multi sector health partnerships can play in addressing the root cause drivers of poor health. We recommend a financing and accountability framework that provides new investment funding for hospitals and their multisector health partners to invest in place based health initiatives. We also recommend that a 100% of the resulting savings be shared for reinvestment. CHA welcomes the opportunity to engage further on a comprehensive solution that will support the cost of care delivery, drive better health, and affordability for all. Thank you.

We have 60 programs throughout the state, group homes, supportive housing, community residents, and serve over 2,500 individuals annually with severe mental illness, developmental disabilities, and substance abuse disorders. Continuum operates residential treatment programs for veterans and emergency shelter with beds for a 100 unhoused individuals. Continuum operates residential programs for DDS and DMAS. And despite similar program types, there is a lack of consistency related to medication management. Within our DDS program, staff are medication certified, enabling them to administer medications. Within the Demus programs, residential staff cannot administer medication. Instead, residential staff working at Demus funded programs monitor this supervision of self administration of medication. In addition to the lack of parity between DDS and DMIS programs, as well as the budgetary impact to Medicaid related to the cost of visiting nurse for medication administration visits, I would like to highlight several key reasons why it would be beneficial to the clinical needs of the residents in Demus funded residential programs. There's a lack of continuity of care, given that visiting nurses are not part of the daily rounds with residential staff and not always able to attend multidisciplinary team meetings. The lack of client centered care and visiting nurses cannot always guarantee to be on time due to crises that occur with previous patients. This often oftentimes results in the residents being late for appointments, day programs, or work due to waiting for their nurse. Residents complain that medications are not taken at their prescribed time due to having to take some of their bedtime medications at seven or 8PM when nurses are able to be present. Due to the utilization review of home care nursing services, residents have medications prepacked for a week at a time, and staff have to monitor those medications. Staff are not always aware of what medications are in, causing a delay if there is a missed or wrong pill. I encourage the committee to consider including review of medication management related to Demus residential programs in this very important bill. Thank you for your consideration.
