[Christie Balaciano]: Good afternoon, representative Gilchrist, senator Lesser, and members of the human services committee. Thank you for the opportunity to provide support for house bill seven one nine one an act concerning Medicaid rate increases, planning, and sustainability. My name is Christie Baliciano. I am the director of marketing communications at Fairhaven Community Healthcare. Fairhaven Community Healthcare is dedicated to providing accessible healthcare services to all individuals in our community. We serve over 36,000 unique patients annually. Greater than 90% of our patients represent minorities, and nearly 70% of our patients are Medicaid recipients. We provide primary care services, including nurse midwifery, dental services, and geriatric care. We are proud to offer dental, behavioral health, care coordination, and substance use services. For literally decades, Connecticut Medicaid has been out of compliance with federal law, which mandates that states reimburse health centers based on allowable costs. Our patients have felt the impact of this practice, though we work diligently to provide high quality care. There is no question that limited funding has translated into limited services. The situation in Connecticut is particularly distressing given the practice in neighboring states, such as Massachusetts, New York, Rhode Island, and Maine. A recent Medicaid study conducted by the Department of Social Services showed that Connecticut health centers receive, on average, 75 to 80% of the fees received by health centers in these neighboring states. Connecticut's reimbursement to health centers are the lowest in New England, the lowest in the Northeast, and among the lowest third across the country. We can do better. Our patients deserve no less. Our center loses over $100 on each Medicaid medical visit and over a $121 on each Medicaid dental visit. We must rely heavily on grants, which are time limited, and on philanthropy, which is often a one time infusion of cash to allow us to continue to provide high quality care our patients deserve. With federal threats to our funding and to Medicaid, together with rising demand for primary care services, ensuring that health centers are paid in compliance with federal law is key to ensuring access to high quality care for all Connecticut residents. At Fairhaven Community Healthcare, we remain committed to serving our community, but we cannot do it alone. It's time for Medicaid rates to reflect the true cost of care, and we urge the committee to support this important bill. Thank you for your time and consideration. Together, we can ensure that all Connecticut residents have access to the care they need and the care they deserve.

[Dr. Khurram Ghuman]: Good afternoon, senator Lester, representative Gilchrist, and distinguished members of the human services committee. My name is Horam Gulman, and I am an independently practicing family physician and the current president of the Connecticut State Medical Society. I'm testifying in support of House Bill 7,191. Medicaid reimbursement is not just a financial issue. It is a health equity issue. Nearly 1,000,000 Connecticut residents rely on Medicaid for their health care, and many are from historically marginalized communities already facing significant barriers to care. Yet Connecticut's Medicare reimbursement rates remain among the lowest in the region directly impacting access to physicians. Specialist wait times are extent excessive. Primary care availability is limited, and mental health care remains out of reach for too many patients. As a physician, I can tell you the doctors want to care for Medicaid patients, but year after year of inadequate reimbursement has made participation unsustainable. Many practices operate as small businesses. And when Medicaid rates do not even cover the cost of providing care, physicians are forced to make difficult choices. As a result, more doctors are leaving the Medicaid network, Medicare reimbursement improves access. Studies confirm that when rates are brought closer to Medicare or private insurance, more physicians participate. Medicare rate study validated what we already knew, and Connecticut's rate are too far far too low compared to the peer states. We must act now. I want to highlight three key areas where this bill can be strengthened. First, all physician specialties and subspecialties must see rate increases, not just those identified in the study. Medicaid access issue in part stems from the years of uneven rate adjustments and leaving out certain specialties will only perpetuate these gaps. Every physician who provides care to Medicaid patients must receive a meaningful increase. Second, while we acknowledge the need for a three year phase in, we urge policymakers to move as quickly as possible. Many physicians have already left the Medicaid network, and once a physician stops accepting Medicaid, it is extremely difficult to bring them back. Delays in implementation will only make rebuilding access more challenging. Third, the bill provides mechanisms for ongoing rate adjustments, but we strongly support using the Medicare economic index rather than the Medicare fee schedule. Medicare rates continue to decline, and the federal policies make them an unstable benchmark. Medicare economic index, however, reflects the actual economic pressures on medical practices, staff wages, rent and supply costs, etcetera, ensuring that Medicaid rates remain viable over time. Finally, no Medicaid rates should be reduced if a payment currently exceeds the 70

I collaborate with community providers to ensure my clients receive the medical and mental health care they need. I help them schedule appointments, arrange transportation, and relay critical clinical information to keep them stable in the community. Education is a key part of my job, teaching them about their diagnoses, medications, and the importance of hygiene and daily living skills. Many of these young adults struggle with medication compliance, which can lead to decompensation and hospitalization. It takes patience, persistence, and a deep understanding of trauma informed care to help them stay engaged in their treatment. Substance use is a significant concern in this population, and I work closely with clients to reduce use and prevent relapse, which can interfere with their progress and lead to legal consequences. For those with criminal histories, I help them adhere to court requirements while ensuring they stay on track with their health and recovery goals. These young adults require nurses with specialized expertise, professionals who can navigate complex cases with compassion, advocacy, and a commitment to long term stability. Without our support, many of them would struggle to access the care they need, increasing the burden on emergency services and the state. Nursing services play a vital role in keeping these clients safe, independent, and moving towards a healthier future. Please consider prioritizing restoration of the 2016 cut to medication administration as you determine your path forward to fund much needed Medicaid provider rate increases. Thank you for your consideration today.

[Kelly Memphis]: Okay. Hello. Good afternoon, chair Lester, representative Gilchrist, ranking member Harding, ranking member Case, and honorable members of the Human Services Committee. My name is Kelly Memphis. I am with the Healthcare Distribution Alliance, HGA. Briefly, HTA is the National Trade Association representing pharmaceutical wholesale distributors. The vital link between around 1,200 pharmaceutical manufacturers and more than 330,000 pharmacies and other healthcare settings nationwide. In Connecticut, our members are responsible for the physical delivery of pharmaceutical products to around 3,200 sites of care. I'm speaking today to respectfully share some concerns that we have with SB 11. We are concerned that this bill misrepresents the role of wholesale distributors, specifically in sections one to five of the bill. First, we are concerned that the definition of wholesale distributor in the bill is overly broad and captures several entities in that definition. And, subsequently, wholesale distributors are then inaccurately captured in the reference rate pricing measures in sections one through five. As you may know, wholesale distributors operate a unique role in the supply chain. In their role as wholesale distributors, they do not set list prices, also known as wholesale acquisition costs or WACC. They do not bring or withdraw products from market, impact, determine pharmacy reimbursements or what patients pay at counters. Rather, wholesale distributors are the logistical experts in the supply chain who, as I mentioned, ensure that pharmaceutical products physically arrive to shelves where and when patients need them. Accordingly, inclusion of wholesale distributors in these pricing measures would cause them to be unduly subject to penalties for decisions they do not determine. This would be especially detrimental in the wholesale, distribution industry, which historically operates on an under 1% net profit margin. So we did wanna share our concerns with that. And while our the inaccurate inclusion of wholesale distributors is our greatest concern, we did just want to express concern of about the pricing measure overall. We are concerned that it could add undue pressure on the generics and pharmaceutical marketplace, which is undergoing a lot of national strain, which could impact the supply of pharmaceuticals in Connecticut and, the availability to patients. We also wish to briefly express our concerns with the wholesale importation piece, which is also reflected in HB seven one nine two. We certainly appreciate the intent of that language, but we are concerned with how it would be compatible with the Drug Supply Chain Security Act. This is a federal law designed to make The US supply chain even more secure. It's a track and trace requirement law, which is in the final stages of a ten year implementation. So we are just concerned about that conflict there. So in conclusion, we do believe that there are some critical amendments needed to SB 11 to ensure that it accurately, represents supply chain and the supply chain can remain resilient in Connecticut. [Rachel Cottle Latham]: Yes. I am. Hello. Good evening, Chairs Lesser, Gilchrist, and members of the Human Services Committee. Thank you for your time. My name is Rachel Cottle Latham. And we support the inclusion of a number of policies in House Bill 5,913 as reflected in our submitted statement. However, PHRMA respectfully opposes several provisions in Senate Bill 11. And I will highlight just a few of these concerns as we provided more comprehensive written testimony. First, this bill would penalize manufacturers for sales exceeding a reference price tied to the consumer price index. This proposal is premised on the idea that drug prices are rising sharply or significantly driving overall spending. But it is well documented that manufacturers are giving larger discounts to insurance companies, middlemen, and hospitals each year, totaling around $334,000,000,000 in 2023 alone. Net prices have increased below the rate of inflation for a fifth year in a row now and are projected to decline by up to 4% through 2028. And, in fact, half of every dollar spent on brand medicines goes to entities that play no role in the research, development, or manufacturing of those medicines. But imposing an arbitrary price cap, arbitrary price cap would penalize manufacturers for increases in list price even where there may be no corresponding increase in net prices. And we respectfully urge the Committee to oppose this provision. In addition, concerning Section seventeen and eighteen of the bill pertaining to the federal three forty program, I want to be clear that PHRMA strongly supports the original intent of the three forty program. However, the program of today has strayed from its true safety net purpose and is in need of systemic reform. Counter to the intent of this bill, Section seventeen and eighteen would lock in exactly what is broken about the 340B program and enshrine in state law problems that are increasing the program's market distortions and increasing profits for PBMs, big for profit chain pharmacies, and large hospitals, as evidenced by a Minnesota Department of Health report finding that contract pharmacies and third party administrators receive around $16 of every $100 of gross 340B revenue. Between 2010 and 2023, in fact, 340B discounted purchases surged from $5 to $66,000,000,000 And contracts between covered entities and pharmacies grew by over 12000% over the same period. But there is scant evidence that these discounts for reaching the patients the program was designed to help. The 340B program has often been touted as cost free to taxpayers, but it is clear that the program is driving up costs for employers, patients, payers, and Connecticut's healthcare system as a whole. Here in Connecticut, employers pay an estimated $121,000,000 more in healthcare costs due to foregone rebates from manufacturers, leading to a $5,900,000 reduction in state and local tax revenue. And finally, it's important to emphasize that a pharmacy's status as a contract pharmacy has no impact on whether or not a patient can pick up their prescriptions, just whether a covered entity receives the 340B discount. [Senator Matt Lesser]: Any other questions or comments for Sarah? Seeing none. Thank you. Next, we have Chris Pankratz followed by James Higgins.

[Joel Norwood]: Good evening, chairpersons, ranking members, and members of the committee. My name is Chris Pankratz, and I'm the CEO and co owner of Masters in Home Care. We're a Connecticut licensed Medicare certified home health care agency. And I also serve as chair of the Government Relations Committee for the Connecticut Association for Health Care at Home. I want to first thank this committee for your efforts to strengthen Medicaid home health care through HB 7,191. To ensure this bill fully supports patients and providers, I urge the committee to consider the following key adjustments: ensuring that all home health services are properly funded. Phase two of the Medicaid rate study failed to include key home health services, most notably the medication administration rate, which per the study accounted for over 50% of Medicaid home health expenditures. Secondly, eliminating the subsequent visit rate penalty. The current structure slashes reimbursement by 50% for multiple patients in the same location, Yet, each patient still requires the same level of care, including documentation, EVV compliance, safety procedures, physician notifications, and care coordination. Removing this penalty ensures that home health agencies can deliver more care without financial penalties. Next, accelerating the Medicaid rate increases. Home health agencies cannot wait until 2028 for sustainable reimbursement. Today, Connecticut's Medicaid rates are 59% lower than Medicare's. I urge the committee to revise the implementation timeline to provide a minimum 10% increase per year for the next four years. And lastly, tying Medicaid rates to inflation beyond 2028. Without an automatic adjustment, we're going to face the same crisis again in a few years. Indexing rates to inflation will ensure long term stability and prevent future funding gaps. Home health care keeps people safe at home, prevents unnecessary hospitalizations, and lowers overall state health care costs. But without adequate reimbursement, agencies will continue limiting Medicaid services, leaving vulnerable residents without care and increasing overall state spending. So I respectfully urge the Committee to strengthen HB 7,191 by accelerating the home health care rate increases, indexing the rates to inflation, ensuring all essential services, including medication administration, are properly funded and included, and eliminating the subsequent visit rate penalty. Thank you for your time, and I welcome any questions and encourage the committee to review my written testimony for additional details.

Emergency Medicaid is an important conduit in addressing coverage gaps for income eligible residents who are systematically excluded from the most affordable health care coverage options including Medicaid and marketplace coverage. SB eight zero six provides a much needed adjustment to expand the conditions eligible for emergency coverage in Connecticut and allows eligible residents to enroll in coverage ahead of any emergencies. This will ensure that uninsured residents have better health outcomes and not be burdened with the administrative tasks to enroll for coverage when facing life threatening chronic conditions. Emergency Medicaid allows states to receive federal matching funds and in light of reduced federal funding through the final allocations of available ARPA funds, as well as threats to cut funding to the Medicaid program, expanding the definition of medical conditions that would qualify under emergency Medicaid coverage will provide the state a pathway to ensure that federal funds can continue to be brought into the state. In our written testimony, we provide recommendations on revisions that we would like to see to SB eight zero six, to clarify some of the current bill language, as well as various conditions under, the emergency Medicaid coverage lists that are covered and other states that are not reflected in the current list provided in SB eight zero six. However, we respectfully recommend that the committee incorporate language that includes that, that includes a plan put forth by the House and Senate leadership that would bring all providers up to seven to at least 75 to 80% of Medicare rates in a multi year plan as this would best address the accessibility and improvements to quality of care across the board for all HESD members and providers. As proposed, this will require 80% of revenue received by nursing home from Medicaid, Medicare, and all other payment sources to be used to pay for nursing personnel who provide hands on care to the residents. Respectfully, LeadingAge Connecticut cannot support this bill. While we highly highly value the work of nurses and nurses' aids, the way this bill is crafted, it is unworkable and does not acknowledge the realities of nursing home care and services. In addition, the nursing home is just that, a home that provides their residents with meals, laundry, linens, hot water, and heat, and all which are expensive and must be paid for by using the revenue received from all these payer sources. You know, regarding the Medicaid revenue, Connecticut has a cost based home nursing home rate system that just transitioned to an acuity based component component, and the new system was designed with staffing concerns in mind. When it comes to Medicare revenue, only the short term rehab patients receive Medicare for their nursing home stay, and the the, rate that the nursing home gets for a Medicaid Medicare patient must cover the cost of physical and occupational therapy, speech therapy, intensive nursing services, and all of their prescription medications.
